# Proposed OEIS comments: A236730 to A240558

One section per OEIS entry: the date the result was obtained, the paper that proves it, what the entry still records as unsettled, and the text proposed for posting on that entry.

**Nothing here has been posted.** See [../SUBMITTING.md](../SUBMITTING.md).

---

## A236730

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A236730>  
Result obtained: **2 September 2026**  
Paper: [7116](../papers/7001-7500/7116-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 9*a(n-1) -9*a(n-2) -109*a(n-3) +254*a(n-4) +260*a(n-5) -1156*a(n-6) +576*a(n-7) +944*a(n-8) -1024*a(n-9) +256*a(n-10)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 22 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 9. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A236737

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A236737>  
Result obtained: **2 September 2026**  
Paper: [287](../papers/0001-0500/0287-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical for column k:

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The recurrences in the linked file are correct. Column k counts arrays of a fixed width, so it is a walk count in a finite graph and satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence; Berlekamp-Massey on exact terms returns the minimal one, its order is the order stated for that column, and so is the order of every tail, so any recurrence of that order is this one. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```


OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A237965>  
Result obtained: **2 September 2026**  
Paper: [437](../papers/0001-0500/0437-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical for column k:

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The recurrences in the linked file are correct. Column k counts arrays of a fixed width, so it is a walk count in a finite graph and satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence; Berlekamp-Massey on exact terms returns the minimal one, its order is the order stated for that column, and so is the order of every tail, so any recurrence of that order is this one. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

Paper: [379](../papers/0001-0500/0379-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical for column k:

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The recurrences in the linked file are correct. Column k counts arrays of a fixed width, so it is a walk count in a finite graph and satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence; Berlekamp-Massey on exact terms returns the minimal one, its order is the order stated for that column, and so is the order of every tail, so any recurrence of that order is this one. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A238807

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A238807>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [2451](../papers/2001-2500/2451-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = (1/360)*n^6 - (1/30)*n^5 + (7/9)*n^4 - (65/12)*n^3 + (11959/360)*n^2 - (1631/20)*n + 83 for n>2.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Oct 24 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 27 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

---

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 3. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A239031

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A239031>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [2838](../papers/2501-3000/2838-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = (1/12)*n^4 - (1/6)*n^3 + (47/12)*n^2 - (29/6)*n + 5.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Oct 25 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 16 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

---

## A239151

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A239151>  
Result obtained: **7 September 2026**  
Paper: [6526](../papers/6501-7000/6526-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 4*a(n-1) +51*a(n-2) -84*a(n-3) -529*a(n-4) +442*a(n-5) +1605*a(n-6) +1916*a(n-7) -5423*a(n-8) -5800*a(n-9) +8753*a(n-10) +890*a(n-11) -5088*a(n-12) +4464*a(n-13) +3456*a(n-14)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. The condition is carried by a bounded amount of state down the array -- one bit per column where it asks for a parity reaching back to the top, and otherwise a window of three consecutive lines, since every cell it names lies one step away -- so counting the arrays a line at a time gives a transfer matrix with 15 states, and a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 13. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A239358

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A239358>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [2974](../papers/2501-3000/2974-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = (7/6)*n^3 - (39/2)*n^2 + (538/3)*n - 486 for n>8.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Oct 25 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 105 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

> Empirical for column k:
> Empirical for row n:

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The recurrences in the linked file are correct. Column k counts arrays of a fixed width, so it is a walk count in a finite graph and satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence; Berlekamp-Massey on exact terms returns the minimal one, its order is the order stated for that column, and so is the order of every tail, so any recurrence of that order is this one. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A239538

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A239538>  
Result obtained: **7 September 2026**  
Paper: [7129](../papers/7001-7500/7129-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 8*a(n-1) +46*a(n-2) -22*a(n-3) -166*a(n-4) +28*a(n-5) +68*a(n-6) +208*a(n-7) -160*a(n-8) -48*a(n-9) +32*a(n-10)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. The condition is carried by a bounded amount of state down the array -- one bit per column where it asks for a parity reaching back to the top, and otherwise a window of three consecutive lines, since every cell it names lies one step away -- so counting the arrays a line at a time gives a transfer matrix with 13 states, and a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 9. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A239844

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A239844>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [2849](../papers/2501-3000/2849-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = (1/24)*n^4 + (1/12)*n^3 + (11/24)*n^2 + (53/12)*n - 6 for n>2.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Oct 26 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 14 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A240333

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A240333>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [2646](../papers/2501-3000/2646-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = (1/30)*n^5 + (1/24)*n^4 + (23/12)*n^3 - (193/24)*n^2 + (581/20)*n - 30 for n>2.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Oct 27 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 22 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A240395

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A240395>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [2812](../papers/2501-3000/2812-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = (1/6)*n^4 - (5/6)*n^3 + (13/3)*n^2 + (31/3)*n - 48 for n>5.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Oct 27 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 26 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A240478

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A240478>  
Result obtained: **7 September 2026**  
Paper: [8632](../papers/8501-9000/8632-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 3*a(n-1) + a(n-2) - 2*a(n-4).
> Empirical g.f.: x*(2 + 2*x - x^2 - 2*x^3) / (1 - 3*x - x^2 + 2*x^4). - _Colin Barker_, Feb 24 2018

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Every cell the condition names lies a bounded number of lines away -- two of them, where it reaches a knight's move -- so a window of that width decides the condition on the line in the middle, and the lines with nothing far enough beyond them are decided at the end. Counting a line at a time therefore gives a transfer matrix with 4 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 4. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A240558

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A240558>  
Result obtained: **25 August 2026**  
Paper: [9590](../papers/9501-10000/9590-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Conjecture: -(n+2)*(n^2-5)*a(n) +8*(-2*n-1)*a(n-1) +16*(n-1)*(n^2+2*n-4)*a(n-2)=0. - _R. J. Mathar_, Jun 14 2016

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
Not only empirical: the generating function given above is algebraic, and a recurrence with polynomial coefficients is a differential operator applied to it. Reducing that operator leaves a polynomial of degree 0, so the recurrence holds for n > 0. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```
